**Playgroup** is a British [dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_music "Dance music") act. It is the project of musician and designer Trevor Jackson.[\[1\]](#cite_note-British_Hit_Singles_&_Albums-1)

They were associated with the [electroclash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electroclash "Electroclash") movement. Jackson has also performed under the names Underdog and Skull.

-   _Playgroup Party mix Vol. 1_ (Playgroup Records, 2001)
-   _Playgroup_ (Source UK, 2001 and on [Astralwerks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astralwerks "Astralwerks") in the USA on 19 March 2002. It was also re-released as a limited edition [double album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_album "Double album") on Playgroup Records in June 2003)

In 2001, Playgroup released the [single](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_\(music\) "Single (music)") "Number One", an up-tempo disco dance song. The [music video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_video "Music video") was computer-animated and featured a tower PC which was transformed into a [humanoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanoid "Humanoid") dancing robot during the song. ### Configure and run skinning and morph target animations in Three.js

Source: https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/examples/webgl_animation_skinning_morph.html

Loads a GLTF character model and configures an AnimationMixer alongside lil-gui to switch skeletal animations and adjust facial morph target influences. Includes smooth transitions between animation states and one-shot emote callbacks with automatic state restoration.

```json

### Create smoke particles, spark embers, and flickering light using TSL

Source: https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js/blob/dev/docs/llms-full.txt

Sets up smoke particles with soft fade-in, spark particles with curl noise displacement and stretched UV glows, and a point light with procedural noise flickering in Three.js.

```javascript
// Smoke starts gently with a fade-in at the bottom

smokeMaterial.blending = THREE.NormalBlending;

**Quick summary:**

The video is Playgroup's "Number One" (2001, Trevor Jackson) — a CGI video of a tower PC transforming into a dancing robot in a digital arena, doing signature moves like Michael Jackson's moonwalk with glowing floor tiles and Angus Young's duckwalk. Early-2000s low-poly aesthetic.

**The good news:** that 2001 CGI style is *easy* for modern WebGL. Three.js can handle everything — skeletal animation, bloom glowing tiles, retro post-FX, the works. The tech is not the bottleneck.

| Approach | Time | What you get |
|----------|------|--------------|
| **Stylized faithful** | **2–3 weeks** | Custom PC-robot model, retargeted Mixamo dance clips, glow floor, music sync, retro look. Recognizable and fun. |
| **Pixel-perfect** | **6–10 weeks** | Frame-matched camera cuts, hand-keyframed every move, exact model proportions, full transformation sequence |

**Stack:** Three.js + GLB assets + Web Audio API for beat sync + post-processing bloom/film grain.

**Hardest part:** There's no pre-made "dancing PC robot" model. Someone has to model and rig it. Animation can be mostly motion-capture retargeting; only the signature moves (moonwalk, duckwalk, PC-to-robot transform) need custom keyframing.

**Skipped:** Raw WebGL/WebGPU (Three.js covers it all), Unity WebGL (too heavy), physics engines (no collision needed). Add Babylon.js only if you want its built-in animation state machine for complex dance choreography.

We have `yt-dlp` and `ffmpeg` installed. Here's my honest assessment of what I can do with video analysis, and the workflow I'd use:

## What I Can Actually Do

**I can't "watch" a video like you do.** I don't have a video player in my head. **But I can analyze the hell out of extracted data:**

- **Screenshots/frames** — I can read image files and describe what I see (poses, colors, camera angles, environment details)
- **Audio** — I can analyze the waveform, detect BPM, find beat drops, segment the track
- **Frame sequences** — I can write scripts to extract frames at intervals, then batch-analyze them
- **Color palettes** — Extract dominant colors per scene for matching the aesthetic
- **Motion analysis** — Compare frames to detect when cuts happen, when the robot moves, etc.

## The Iteration Workflow

Here's how I'd build this in layers:

### Phase 1: Reference Extraction (automated)
```bash
# Download video
yt-dlp "https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IJKLb1TgmXY" -o "reference.mp4"

# Extract frames every 2 seconds for scene analysis
ffmpeg -i reference.mp4 -vf "fps=1/2" frames/frame_%04d.png

# Extract audio for BPM/beat analysis
ffmpeg -i reference.mp4 -vn -acodec pcm_s16le -ar 44100 -ac 2 audio.wav

# Detect scene cuts
ffmpeg -i reference.mp4 -filter:v "select='gt(scene,0.3)',showinfo" -f null -
```

### Phase 2: Manual Analysis (me looking at frames)
I'd read key frames and build a **shot list** — e.g.:
- 0:00–0:05: PC tower, dark background, blue glow
- 0:06–0:12: Transformation begins, monitor swivels up
- 0:15: Robot stands, camera low angle
- etc.

I'd note camera positions, lighting colors, robot pose per shot.

### Phase 3: Build the Basic Version
- Single robot model (simple boxes + cylinders for PC parts)
- 3–4 static poses matching key frames
- Basic arena (grid floor, colored lights)
- No animation yet — just a rotatable 3D scene that looks like the video

### Phase 4: Add Animation Layer by Layer
1. **Idle/bounce** — robot bobs to a simple sine wave
2. **Walk cycle** — forward/backward movement
3. **Signature moves** — moonwalk, duckwalk as individual clips
4. **Camera cuts** — switch between preset camera angles
5. **Transformation** — the PC-to-robot morph
6. **Music sync** — tie everything to beat detection

Each phase gives you something you can look at and decide "good enough" or "keep going."

## Tools That Would Help

| Tool | What it does | How I'd use it |
|------|-----------|--------------|
| **ffmpeg** | Video swiss army knife | Frame extraction, audio extraction, scene detection, thumbnail generation |
| **yt-dlp** | YouTube downloader | Grab the reference video |
| **aubio** / **librosa** (Python) | Audio analysis | BPM detection, beat tracking, onset detection for sync |
| **OpenPose** or **MediaPipe** | Pose estimation | If we wanted to reverse-engineer dance moves from video frames (overkill for this) |
| **Three.js editor** | Visual scene editor | Place lights, camera, preview models without coding |

## The Honest Limitation

The **biggest bottleneck isn't the tech — it's the reference material.** If the video is low-res, dark, or the robot moves fast between frames, my frame analysis won't catch fine details. I'd need you to say things like "the robot's head is a CRT monitor, not flatscreen" or "the floor tiles are purple, not blue."

## What I Propose

1. **You grab the video** (or give me a link) — I'll download it and extract the frame/audio data
2. **I'll analyze 20–30 key frames** and build a shot list + color palette
3. **I'll build Phase 1** — a static Three.js scene that looks roughly right
4. **You look at it** and tell me what's wrong ("robot too tall", "lights too dim")
5. **I iterate** — add animation, fix proportions, add effects

Want to try it? Give me the YouTube URL (or any video file you have) and I'll start the extraction pipeline right now. I'll show you exactly what the frame analysis looks like.

Let me be careful with the code and get it right first time. Key API points for three 0.186:

- `THREE.WebGLRenderer({ antialias: true })`
- `renderer.outputColorSpace` defaults to SRGBColorSpace — but with OutputPass in the chain, the OutputPass handles conversion. When using EffectComposer, the composer render targets should be in linear space and the OutputPass does tone mapping + sRGB. Set `renderer.toneMapping` before, and OutputPass picks it up.

Actually, in recent three, the recommended pattern:
```js
const composer = new EffectComposer(renderer);
composer.addPass(new RenderPass(scene, camera));
composer.addPass(bloomPass);
composer.addPass(new OutputPass());
```
And `renderer.toneMapping = THREE.ACESFilmicToneMapping` — OutputPass reads `renderer.toneMapping`. Yes, OutputPass applies the renderer's tone mapping and output color space.

- `PMREMGenerator`: 
```js
const pmrem = new THREE.PMREMGenerator(renderer);
scene.environment = pmrem.fromScene(new RoomEnvironment(), 0.04).texture;
```

To make the form read like the video (which shows a soft gradient across the surfaces — brighter on top, darker toward the bottom), I need:

Best: use a custom gradient environment. I can build one cheaply with a `DataTexture` / canvas equirect gradient, run through PMREM. ~15 lines. That gives me full control over the look: bright top, mid cream, darker bottom. This is exactly how product renders get that look.

Let me do that: replace RoomEnvironment with a gradient equirect texture.

```js
function studioEnv() {
  const c = document.createElement('canvas');
  c.width = 32; c.height = 256;           // vertical gradient, equirect
  const g = c.getContext('2d');
  const grad = g.createLinearGradient(0, 0, 0, 256);
  grad.addColorStop(0.00, '#ffffff');     // overhead: a big soft softbox
  grad.addColorStop(0.42, '#f2eee6');
  grad.addColorStop(0.50, '#dedad2');     // horizon
  grad.addColorStop(0.62, '#b9b4aa');     // floor bounce
  grad.addColorStop(1.00, '#6f6b64');     // dark under
  g.fillStyle = grad;
  g.fillRect(0, 0, 32, 256);
  const t = new THREE.CanvasTexture(c);
  t.mapping = THREE.EquirectangularReflectionMapping;
  t.colorSpace = THREE.SRGBColorSpace;
  return t;
}
```

Then PMREM it:
```js
const pmrem = new THREE.PMREMGenerator(renderer);
scene.environment = pmrem.fromEquirectangular(studioEnv()).texture;
```

That gives a studio gradient environment — brushed metal will show a vertical light-to-dark gradient, exactly like the video's surfaces.

Three things cost me render cycles and are worth knowing, all recorded in `README.md`:

- **Light intensities need to be ~3× intuition.** three.js r155+ is physically correct and the Lambert BRDF carries a 1/π. I had the whole scene at half brightness until I spotted it.
- **ACES tone mapping is the wrong tool here.** It crushed this studio setup to muddy grey. `NeutralToneMapping` is built for bright, flat, colour-accurate work.
- **`anisotropy` is UV-scale dependent.** The two stacked boxes shared a 0–1 UV across a 3.6× size difference, so they rendered at visibly different roughness and the seam looked like two separate objects. Dropped it.

Also corrected the record: the inherited summary had the hand/power-button details misattributed, and described the "cityscape" as abstract architecture. It's actually a **spectrum analyser** — EQ bars rising from the metal floor.

Skipped: Act 1's animated beats (tray sliding out, top section rising, the fly-through), depth-of-field pass, and the brushing grain. Add those when you've reviewed the static model — proportions and material are the things worth your eye first.

`shots/act1.png` is the current render if you want to compare it against `reference/extracted/frames/frame_0003.jpg` before loading it up.

Let me write a small tracked-text helper.

```js
  const tracked = (text, cx, y, tracking) => {
    const widths = [...text].map(ch => g.measureText(ch).width);
    const total = widths.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0) + tracking * (text.length - 1);
    let x = cx - total / 2;
    [...text].forEach((ch, i) => { g.fillText(ch, x, y); x += widths[i] + tracking; });
  };
```

#### ← result `read`

crushes mid-tones and desaturates; it rendered this studio setup as muddy grey.
Neutral (Khronos PBR Neutral) is designed for bright, flat, colour-accurate work.

**Don't use `anisotropy` on a material shared by boxes of different sizes.** It is
UV-scale dependent, so two stacked boxes sharing a 0–1 UV rendered at visibly
different roughness and the seam read as two separate objects.

**A metal box's front face reflects the *horizon* band of its environment.** With
`RoomEnvironment` (a uniform white box) a `metalness: 1` body renders flat white with
no gradient at all. The scene uses a hand-built vertical gradient env instead —
bright at the top, falling off below — and keeps `metalness` at 0.45 so the key
light can still shape the form.

**Framing:** the video's Act 1 is near-white-on-white (frame 1 is almost pure blown
out). The scene is deliberately exposed to the much more readable frames 2–3 so the
model can actually be reviewed. `renderer.toneMappingExposure` at ~1.6 reproduces
the frame-1 look.
**Reflections are load-bearing.** The cube sits on a true planar mirror in every
Act 1 shot and again in the closing frame. A glossy floor material won't give you
this — it reflects the environment, not the object. Use `Reflector` plus a
`ShadowMaterial` plane just above it to keep the contact shadow.

**Read the video with contact sheets, not sparse frames.** 2s-interval frames miss
most of Act 1's beats entirely. `ffmpeg -ss 0 -t 12 -i reference/video.mp4 -vf
"fps=2,scale=320:-1,tile=4x6" -frames:v 1 out.png` puts 24 frames in one image —
cheap to review and it made the CD insertion and the lid-lift obvious immediately.

## Known gaps

- Act 1's animated beats not done: CD insert, lid lifting/hinging, EQ bars rising
- No speaker-driver model (Act 1, 19s–28s) — a significant asset
- No depth-of-field pass (bloom + fog only approximates it)
- Brushed grain isn't visible; needs a roughness map
- Front-panel proportions are a best reading of a macro shot — the panel may not
  cover the full front face; needs a wider reference view to confirm

**The keychain prompt was a symptom, not the cause.** `--use-mock-keychain` addresses it, but the real problem is that the full Chrome for Testing app bundle wedges under load — it spawns helper processes, does a Keychain lookup, and attempts GCM registration, any of which can leave it never exiting. It was hanging on `--dump-dom about:blank` and writing nothing. The fix is to use **`chrome-headless-shell`**, the purpose-built binary sitting right next to it in the Playwright cache: no keychain, no profile prompts, exits cleanly.

`tools/shoot.sh` now wraps that, starts and reaps its own server, and takes a `WAIT` env var so a loaded machine doesn't look like a code failure. Same command took **8s and 90s** on consecutive runs, so if a render hangs, that's machine load — not the page.

Act 1 is animated. The full sequence runs: cube on the mirror floor with a red LED → CD slides into the slot → LED green → camera pitches down and dollies onto the slot until it fills the frame → halves separate along it → camera pulls back as the lid hinges open about its rear edge.

**Controls:** space play/pause, ← → to step (±0.05s, shift for ±0.5s), **O** for free orbit, scrub bar at the bottom. `?t=6.2` jumps straight to a beat and holds it, which is how I've been taking these stills. `shots/beats/sheet.png` has four beats.

Rule's in `AGENTS.md`, with the receipts:

> **When in doubt about a movement or shape, grab more frames between the two that are confusing you.** Do not reason harder from the frames you already have, and do not guess.
>
> This is not a fallback for when analysis fails — it is the *first* move.

It's got the table of every wrong turn on this project and what fixed it, plus the two ffmpeg commands. I also added: flag uncertainty as *"I can't read this at this resolution"* rather than writing a guess into code with a soft "unverified" comment — that's why the invented `TREVOR JACKSON` deboss sat in the build for several rounds.

Three bugs found and fixed along the way, all now in the README:

- **The groove slab became a black rectangle.** The dark box filling the CD slot was fine while closed; when the lid hinged open it turned its 275mm top face to the camera. `BoxGeometry` takes one material per face, so the edges stay dark (what you see *through* the slot) and the broad faces take the body material (what you see when it *opens*).
- **`panelLower` had been parenting to an offset mesh**, silently adding `baseH/2` again — `PLAYGROUP` was sitting ~70mm too high, crammed against the slot. Both halves are now origin-anchored Groups.
- **The slot was 36px thick at the close-up**, because `RoundedBoxGeometry` rounds the two facing edges as well, making the visible band `gap + 2R`. R is down to 1.8mm as compensation.

The two most speculative things I'd flag: that R compromise makes the cube's **outer** edges slightly crisper than the reference (proper fix is flat facing edges, which belongs with modelling the interior), and **there's no interior** — open the lid and you see the two mating faces and nothing else, where the reference shows internal panels.

Next natural steps are the EQ bars rising from the floor, then the speaker-driver sequence at 19s–28s, which is a whole asset that isn't started.

Plan:

2-link IK in 2D:
Given anchor A, target T, lengths L1, L2:
- d = |T - A|
- clamp d to [|L1-L2|, L1+L2]
- a = (L1² - L2² + d²) / (2d)
- h = sqrt(L1² - a²)
- midpoint M = A + a * (T-A)/d
- elbow = M ± h * perpendicular(T-A)/d

Choose the sign for the elbow to be "up" (inverted V).

Then:
- Link A: a cylinder from A to the elbow
- Link B: a cylinder from the elbow to T

To orient a link mesh between two points: position at the midpoint, and set rotation to align the local Y axis (for a cylinder) with the direction. Use `quaternion.setFromUnitVectors(new Vector3(0,1,0), dir)`.

Fixed. You were right on both counts, and the second one was the more useful correction.

**The split beat.** The split starts at **6.5s, not 7.0** — and the camera held tight through it, so you watch the gap open in close-up before anything widens. My cut at 7.06 threw that away entirely. The pull-back also **eases over ~0.4s**; only the two moments at 2.2s and in the drive-tray area are hard cuts.

**On which note — the measurement found something I'd have kept missing.** After the split the video's camera *never settles*. Its object coverage runs 60% → 60% → 69% → **100%** → 100%. It pulls back to a medium shot, then **pushes straight back in** to a full-frame close-up of the mechanism. I'd built it as "pull back once and hold," which is why my coverage sat flat at 43% while the video's climbed to 100%. Pushing in isn't decoration there, it's the shot.

**I stopped eyeballing the framing.** `tools/framing.py` high-passes both frames, isolates the machine, and reports coverage, a zoom ratio, and vertical centre offset. Two iterations took 7.3s / 7.9s / 8.5s from 25% off to **within 5%**. It also caught that coverage matching ≠ angle matching — two cameras can put identical pixels on an object at very different elevations, which is why my first "converged" attempt still showed huge top faces against the video's thin slivers.

I put your point in `AGENTS.md` as a standing rule, since it had already cost real time:

> **The reference is CGI from 2001 made by animators taking liberties. Match the video, not physics.** ... Being "correct" and looking different is a worse outcome than being slightly impossible and matching the frame.
>
> This has already cost time: a two-link scissor was constrained by what it could physically reach, which fought directly against making it look like the reference.

So the arm is now free to be rebuilt purely for looks, and I've flagged that in the known gaps rather than defending its current geometry.

`shots/cmp/full.png` is the six-beat sweep.

**Still genuinely open:** the mechanism at 8.5–9.9. The video's closing shots are full-frame close-ups where the chrome wedge ends up standing proud of a *closed* box; mine hides the arm when shut, since a folded linkage interpenetrates the solid halves. The reference's actual linkage still isn't clear to me at this resolution — that's the one thing I'd want a tighter crop of 9.5–10.5s for before rebuilding, rather than guessing again.

Yes — and the key realisation is that most of what I've been burning tokens on is **not visual**. "Where is the object, how big, what shape, when does it move" is all measurable. Only "what *is* that thing" needs eyes.

Let me check what I'd have to work with, because it changes the design:

- **PIL is the only scientific library present** — no numpy, scipy, cv2, or matplotlib
- **ffmpeg has `scdet`** — proper scene-cut detection, which I've been hacking badly with the `select` filter
- **No browser automation** — so the render side has a hidden second bottleneck (below)

## The abstraction: work with silhouettes, not pixels

Extract the object mask per frame (high-pass + threshold — the studio background drops out), then everything reduces to a handful of numbers:

| Measure | Cost | Gives |
|---|---|---|
| `getbbox()` | C-speed | position + scale |
| row/col profiles via `resize((1,H))` / `resize((W,1))` | C-speed | centroid, spread, orientation |
| `ImageChops.logical_and/or` on mode `'1'` | C-speed | **IoU** |
| same, after normalising each bbox | C-speed | **shape-only match** |

That last pair is the important one. Split into three numbers — *shape* error (after normalising position and scale), *position* error, *scale* error — and a bad score tells me which of the three is wrong without looking at anything.

My check above was worth doing: I'd assumed numpy was needed. It isn't. `resize` to a 1-pixel axis does the averaging in C, and mode-`'1'` boolean ops are C too, so **pure PIL gets there with no new dependency**. I'd verify the `resize` averaging empirically before relying on it.

## The second bottleneck nobody's mentioned

Each render currently costs a browser launch — 8–20s, and I've been doing **one frame per launch**. At 30fps for the dance act that's unusable regardless of token cost.

Fix: a `?bake=64&t0=..&t1=..` mode where the page renders N frames into one offscreen canvas and exposes *that* as the screenshot. One launch, 64 frames. Needs `preserveDrawingBuffer: true` for reliable readback. This is probably worth more than the analysis side.

## Proposed shape

```
tools/shots.py      ffmpeg scdet -> exact cut list
tools/profile.py    video or frames -> per-frame CSV (bbox, centroid, area, spread)
tools/bake          page mode: N frames, one launch
tools/diff.py       ref vs render -> IoU / shape / position / scale table
                    + a montage of ONLY the worst-scoring frames
```

Then the loop becomes:

```
edit → bake → diff → read the 2 worst frames instead of all of them
```

Same tooling covers Act 3 for free: motion-energy curves, limb-extreme tracking, and beat alignment — the last via `ffmpeg -ar 100 -ac 1` PCM into a pure-Python onset detector, ~40 lines, no deps.

## What it won't do

It won't tell me a white semicircle is a CD, or that a deboss reads `PLAYGROUP`. Semantics still need eyes. But *"my render is 12% too small, 40px low, and the silhouette is wrong after normalising"* is fully automatable — and that's the loop I've been spending tokens on for the last several rounds.

And `runBake`:
```js
function runBake(n) {
  const t0 = parseFloat(params.get('t0') ?? 0);
  const t1 = parseFloat(params.get('t1') ?? TL.duration);
  const cols = parseInt(params.get('cols') ?? '8', 10);
  const w = parseInt(params.get('w') ?? '384', 10);
  const h = parseInt(params.get('h') ?? '216', 10);
  const rows = Math.ceil(n / cols);

  renderer.setPixelRatio(1);
  renderer.setSize(w, h, false);
  composer.setSize(w, h);
  camera.aspect = w / h;
  camera.updateProjectionMatrix();

  // hide UI
  document.getElementById('hud').style.display = 'none';
  document.getElementById('strip').style.display = 'none';

  const sheet = document.createElement('canvas');
  sheet.width = cols * w;
  sheet.height = rows * h;
  const ctx = sheet.getContext('2d');

  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    const t = n === 1 ? t0 : t0 + (t1 - t0) * (i / (n - 1));
    apply(t);
    composer.render();
    ctx.drawImage(renderer.domElement, (i % cols) * w, Math.floor(i / cols) * h, w, h);
  }

  // replace the page with the sheet
  document.body.innerHTML = '';
  document.body.style.margin = '0';
  document.body.style.overflow = 'hidden';
  sheet.style.display = 'block';
  document.body.appendChild(sheet);
  document.title = `bake ${n} ${cols}x${rows} ${sheet.width}x${sheet.height}`;
}
```

def fit(mask, size=128):
    bbox = mask.getbbox()
    if not bbox: return None
    m = mask.crop(bbox)
    w, h = m.size
    s = size / max(w, h)
    nw, nh = max(1, round(w*s)), max(1, round(h*s))
    m = m.resize((nw, nh), Image.NEAREST)
    canvas = Image.new('1', (size, size), 0)
    canvas.paste(m, ((size-nw)//2, (size-nh)//2))
    return canvas

Let me write the improved mask_of:

```python
def mask_of(im, thresh):
    g = im.convert("L")
    hp = ImageChops.difference(g, g.filter(ImageFilter.GaussianBlur(14)))
    m = hp.point(lambda v: 255 if v > thresh else 0).convert("1")
    # close small gaps so the outline encloses a region
    m = m.convert("L").filter(ImageFilter.MaxFilter(5)).filter(ImageFilter.MinFilter(5)).convert("1")
    # fill holes: flood the outside, anything left white in the inverse is interior
    inv = ImageChops.invert(m).convert("L")
    ImageDraw.floodfill(inv, (0, 0), 0)
    return ImageChops.lighter(m.convert("L"), inv).point(lambda v: 255 if v > 127 else 0).convert("1")
```

```python
def otsu(g):
    h = g.histogram()[:256]
    total = sum(h)
    sum_all = sum(i*h[i] for i in range(256))
    sumB = 0.0; wB = 0; best = 0; thr = 128
    for i in range(256):
        wB += h[i]
        if wB == 0: continue
        wF = total - wB
        if wF == 0: break
        sumB += i*h[i]
        mB = sumB/wB; mF = (sum_all-sumB)/wF
        var = wB*wF*(mB-mF)**2
        if var > best: best = var; thr = i
    return thr
```

Then:
```python
def mask_of(im, _thresh=None):
    g = im.convert('L').resize(ANALYSIS, Image.BOX)
    t = otsu(g) if _thresh is None else _thresh
    m = g.point(lambda v: 255 if v > t else 0).convert('1')
    # foreground = smaller class
    n = count_on(m)
    if n > (ANALYSIS[0]*ANALYSIS[1])/2:
        m = ImageChops.invert(m)
    return m
```

ANALYSIS = (320, 180)   # all masking happens at this resolution


def mask_of(im, thresh):
    """Solid silhouette, computed at a COMMON analysis resolution.

    Two things here are load-bearing and were both got wrong first:

    1. Stopping at high-pass + threshold yields an EDGE map, not a silhouette —
       only a few tenths of a percent of the frame — so IoU between two of them is
       near zero however well the render matches. Close the outline, fill inside.

    2. Masking at native resolution makes the video's grain register as object. Its
       masks then covered ~1.6x ours systematically, purely because video is soft and
       a render is crisp. Downsampling both to a common grid first puts them on
       comparable footing.
    """
    g = im.convert("L").resize(ANALYSIS, Image.BOX)
    hp = ImageChops.difference(g, g.filter(ImageFilter.GaussianBlur(9)))
    # Threshold ADAPTIVELY, in units of this image's own detail contrast. An absolute
    # threshold is not comparable across sources: a crisp render has far higher
    # high-frequency energy than soft, grainy video, so its masks came out ~1.7x
    # bigger on every frame — and a slim mask compared against a fat one can never
    # score a good IoU however well the shapes correspond.
    #
    # Use the MEAN of the high-pass, which is the mean absolute deviation and thus a
    # valid scale proxy. Do NOT use a second moment here: ImageChops.multiply
    # divides by 255, so "variance" computed that way collapses to ~0 and the
    # threshold silently becomes a constant.
    mad = hp.resize((1, 1), Image.BOX).getpixel((0, 0))
    t = max(2.0, thresh * mad)
    m = hp.point(lambda v: 255 if v > t else 0).convert("1")

    m = (m.convert("L")
         .filter(ImageFilter.MaxFilter(5))
         .filter(ImageFilter.MinFilter(5)))

    # fill holes: flood the outside, then whatever is still white in the inverse
    # is interior. ImageDraw.floodfill is the only flood fill PIL exposes.
    inv = ImageChops.invert(m.convert("1")).convert("L")

def mask_otsu(im): ...
def mask_edge(im): ...
def masks_for(ref, mine): 
    """Try both segmentations; pick the one where both coverages are plausible
    and agree. Report which."""
    cands = []
    for mode, fn in (('otsu', mask_otsu), ('edge', mask_edge)):
        a, b = fn(ref), fn(mine)
        ca, cb = cov(a), cov(b)
        sim = abs(ca-cb)
        plausible = 8 <= min(ca,cb) and max(ca,cb) <= 92
        cands.append((plausible, sim, mode, a, b, ca, cb))
    # prefer plausible, then smallest coverage difference
    cands.sort(key=lambda c: (not c[0], c[1]))
    return cands[0]
```

## ASSISTANT  
`2026-09-27 05:39:32Z`


Built and verified. The loop is now **8 frames measured in 8 seconds**, one browser launch, zero image reads:

```sh
tools/diff.py 1.0 2.8 6.9 7.3 8.5     # bake + measure
tools/diff.py 7.3 7.9 --reuse          # re-measure, no re-bake
tools/diff.py 1.0 2.8 --selftest       # verify the instrument
```

## The bottleneck wasn't tokens

It was **one browser launch per frame**. `?bakeTs=` now renders a list of timeline positions into a single canvas, so 8 frames cost one launch instead of eight. That alone is the difference between a 60-frame sweep taking 20 minutes and 40 seconds.

## I had to throw away my first two segmentation designs

Worth recording because both failed *silently*, which is the dangerous kind:

1. **High-pass + threshold** produces an *edge map*, not a silhouette — 0.2% of the frame. IoU between two thin outlines is near zero no matter how well they match.
2. **Adding closing + hole-fill** then made it merely *sometimes* right. The mask dumping showed it returning the **dark background instead of the lit robot** on some frames. Topological fill on an edge map has no reliable notion of which side is the subject.

What actually works is boring: **threshold by luminance** (Otsu) and take the smaller class as the subject. A bright robot on a dark floor is a thresholding problem, not an edge problem.

But Otsu is wrong for Act 1, where the machine is white-on-white and it just splits noise — while the edge method scored **0.98** there. So `pick_masks()` runs both and keeps whichever is plausible, reporting which it chose. That's why the table has a `mode` column.

## The check that makes the rest trustworthy

`--selftest` compares each frame against **itself**. Every score must be 1.00 — and it is, on all nine frames. So the poor ref-vs-render numbers are real differences, not measurement artifacts. Two earlier versions would have handed me confident nonsense.

It's in `AGENTS.md` as non-optional before trusting a reading.

## Honest limits

Act 1's mean `iou 0.32 / shape 0.33` is **mostly not actionable** — two of eight frames are flagged `MASK UNRELIABLE`, and white-on-white studio shots genuinely don't segment. That's fine: Act 1 is judged by eye against contact sheets, and the metric earns its keep from Act 3 onward, where I validated it on real dance frames and the masks correctly land on the robot.

One caveat I'd flag: at t=90 the mask also captures the spotlight pool on the floor, not the robot alone. Fine for ref-vs-render comparison since both will have a pool, but it's not a robot-only silhouette. If limb tracking needs that, it'll need more than global thresholding.

`shots/diff/worst.png` auto-montages only the worst-scoring frames, so a visual check costs one image read instead of a sweep.

**What SAM is good at:**
- Promptable segmentation: given a point, box, or mask, it produces a high-quality object mask
- Zero-shot, general purpose, works on arbitrary objects
- Handles low-contrast boundaries well (it uses strong learned priors)
- SAM 2 does video with temporal propagation

2. The mask at t=90 includes the light pool — SAM prompted on the robot would give just the robot. **That's a real fix.**

```python
def segment_reference(ref, target_cov):
    """Segment the reference, choosing between candidate methods by which lands
    closest to the KNOWN coverage of our rendered silhouette."""
    best = None
    for mode, fn in MODES:
        a = fn(ref)
        ca = count_on(a)/area*100
        d = abs(ca - target_cov)
        if best is None or d < best[0]:
            best = (d, mode, a, ca)
    return best[1], best[2], best[3], best[0] <= 8
```

## ASSISTANT  
`2026-09-27 05:42:26Z`


Yes — but it's the *second* most valuable thing here, and the more valuable one turned out to be free.

**Our side never needed segmentation.** Bake mode now emits two bands: beauty on top, an exact **silhouette pass** below. `scene.overrideMaterial` flat white, black background, no composer, mirror and shadow-catcher excluded. We authored the scene — we already know what's visible. It's exact, costs ~20 lines, and it excludes the floor reflection for free, which no threshold can do. That halves the problem.

It also gave us a way to disambiguate the other half: our coverage is now known exactly, so `segment_reference()` picks whichever reference segmentation lands closest to it.

**Where SAM would genuinely help** — all on the reference side:

- **Act 1's white-on-white.** No threshold can work when the machine is the same value as the void. A learned objectness prior could.
- **Robot vs spotlight pool.** At t=90 my otsu mask grabs both. That needs semantics, not a threshold. Fine for ref-vs-render (both will have a pool), useless for limb tracking.
- **SAM 2's video propagation** — the strongest argument. It keeps *one object identity* across frames, so motion curves don't flicker between independently-segmented frames. Per-frame segmentation has no memory, and the dance act is exactly where that bites.
- **Point prompts on joints**, which is beyond any global threshold.

**Cost:** PyTorch (~2.5GB) plus a checkpoint. Worth being clear that this is a **dev-only** dependency — what we ship stays a single HTML file with no build step.

**My recommendation: not yet.** Classical CV is adequate where it matters most (bright robot, dark arena), Act 1 is judged by eye against contact sheets anyway, and adding SAM before a real Act 3 frame defeats it would be speculative. Add it when a specific frame defeats the current tool, not in anticipation.

I'd also flag the bigger risk plainly: **the tooling is already deep enough to be a rabbit hole.** I've spent a lot of this session on it, and it exists to serve the recreation, not the other way round. Worth measuring, but not worth pre-emptively building a neural pipeline for.

One caveat on today's numbers: Act 1's `mean iou 0.35` is still mostly reference-side artifact — at t=6.9 the render silhouette fills 98% of the frame while the reference segmentation finds only 68%. Trust the render-side numbers now; treat the reference side as approximate until Act 3 justifies a better segmenter.

Let me think about this really carefully, because it's a genuine planning question and I should give a substantive, honest answer rather than just agreeing.

**What are the "stages" of the model here?**

Looking at the video's arc (from the shot list):
1. **Act 1**: The machine as a cube — closed, then splitting, then the lid lifting on the arm. Also: the EQ bars, the speaker driver.
2. **Act 2**: The machine disassembles into components; the robot assembles from those parts (torso = cube, arms, legs, pelvis dome, feet).
3. **Act 3**: The robot dancing — needs a full articulated rig with joints at shoulders, elbows, hips, knees, ankles, waist.
4. **Act 4**: Back to a closed cube.

So the "model" progresses: cube → opening cube + arm → disassembled parts → full humanoid rig → cube again.

**The question: model first, or animate as we go?**

Let me think about the real trade-offs.

**Arguments FOR modeling first (build the full rig, then animate):**
1. **Retargeting/rig stability.** If the robot's joint hierarchy changes (pivot positions, limb proportions), every animation curve keyed against the old rig breaks or needs re-tuning. Hand-keyed dance choreography is expensive; redoing it because a shoulder pivot moved is brutal.
2. **Animation reveals modeling errors late.** If I animate the moonwalk and only then discover the leg proportions are wrong, I've wasted the choreography work.
3. **The reference gives proportions early.** I can measure the robot's proportions from the video NOW (contact sheets, the assembled robot at t=35-45, 65, 75, 90). So I can build the rig to correct proportions up front.
4. **Shared assets.** The cube IS the torso. The arm mechanism is presumably the ancestor of the robot's arms. Getting those right once avoids rework.
5. **Programmatic animation needs stable joints.** If I drive the dance with procedural motion (sinusoids, IK), the joint structure must be settled.

**Arguments AGAINST (animate incrementally as now):**
1. **You don't know what the animation needs until you animate.** E.g., I only discovered the lid pivot needed to be at the centre (not the rear edge) by animating it. Model-first would likely have got that wrong too.
2. **Feedback loop.** Each stage rendered and compared against the reference validates the model. Building a huge rig untested risks large-scale errors.
3. **The reference itself is progressively revealed.** Act 3's robot is only visible in certain shots; I've barely looked at those frames in detail. Modeling the whole robot now means modeling from a few frames.
4. **YAGNI / ponytail**: don't build the leg rig until there's a leg to animate.

**Answering "model before animation?"**

The evidence:

- The rig will need many joints (hips ×2, knees ×2, ankles ×2, shoulders ×2, elbows ×2, wrists ×2, waist, neck)
- The dance choreography is expensive hand-keyed data

So: **yes, model the rig before the dance — but not before Act 1.**

The reasoning:
1. Act 1's animation is *procedural* (numbers over t). Changing the model there is cheap. So keep iterating Act 1 as now — model-first would be premature.
2. Act 3's animation is *hand-keyed choreography* — expensive, and it breaks whenever a pivot moves. That must come after the rig is settled.
3. The dividing line is **how expensive the animation on top is**, not which act you're in.

Also worth noting: the rig needs correct PIVOTS, and I've already been bitten by that (the lid pivot). For a dancing robot, wrong pivots make every pose wrong and you discover it pose-by-pose.

## My answer: yes for the robot rig — but the dividing line isn't "model vs animate"

It's **how expensive is the animation sitting on top of a given part.**

Act 1's animation is **procedural** — tracks of numbers over `t`. Changing the model there is nearly free, which is exactly why the current incremental approach has been right: I've already moved the lid pivot, rebuilt the arm, and relocated `PLAYGROUP` without losing any work. Model-first would have been premature *and probably wrong* — I only discovered the lid pivot needed to be at the centre by animating it.

Act 3's dance is **hand-keyed choreography**: expensive, and it breaks the moment a pivot moves. That absolutely must be authored against a settled rig.

## The distinction that matters: pivots vs surface detail

- **Surface detail doesn't break animation.** The flat panels, the orange cables, the chrome ball joints, the fan on the cube's back — all can be added or refined *after* choreography is keyed, because they don't move joint centres.
- **Pivots and proportions do break animation.** A shoulder pivot 15mm off makes every pose wrong, and you find out pose by pose. That's the expensive failure mode.

So the gate isn't "finish the model" — it's **finish the blockout rig.**

## Proposed order

1. **Finish Act 1's remaining beats** — cheap, procedural, no rig dependency
2. **Blockout rig** — correct proportions and joint pivots measured from the reference, simple boxes for limbs
3. **Pose harness** — `?pose=shoulderL:45,elbowL:-30&...` renders a static pose; diff it against a reference frame
4. **Verify** against frames 60 (arms raised), 75 (duckwalk), 90 (moonwalk). If a static pose matches, the rig is right
5. **Key the choreography** — the expensive part, now safe
6. **Surface detail** — panels, cables, fan — whenever, without touching animation

Step 3 is the same discipline as the camera calibration: make it measurable, verify numerically, then build on it. It reuses the bake/diff tooling as-is, and it's a small addition — a URL param that applies a joint-angle map instead of the timeline.

One cost worth naming honestly: steps 2–4 are a stretch with **no visible progress** — just a blocky robot being posed and diffed. That's the price of not re-keying a dance later.

Blockout rig is in and verified. `[`shots/rig/landmarks.png`](shots/rig/landmarks.png)` is the check — measured landmark fractions overlaid on the reference and the blockout, and the joints land close.

## What's built

`?rig=1` shows the robot, hides Act 1. Proportions measured off a real full-body frame (`shots/rig/robot_120.5.png`), stored as **fractions of total height** so absolute scale can change freely.

**Pose harness works** — every joint articulates:
```sh
?rig=1&pose=shoulderL.z=58,shoulderR.z=-58,elbowL.x=-28,hipL.x=-22,kneeL.x=42
?rig=1&angle=40          # orbit the figure
```

That's the gate I argued for: **pivots are what break animation**, and finding out while the pose is static is far cheaper than after a dance is keyed.

## Three bugs worth noting

- **The head floated 1.2m above a 2.2m robot** — I used an absolute height fraction where a parent-relative offset was needed. Every local offset is now an explicit *difference* of absolute fractions.
- **The arms were 2.7× too short.** The reference arm runs shoulder 0.787 → elbow 0.625 → hand 0.456; I'd guessed ~0.12H instead of 0.16H + 0.17H.
- **`Number('') === 0`**, so an *absent* `bakeTs` silently put the page into bake mode — the canvas was a 384×216 tile in the corner of the window.

## Known deviations (all surface, not pivot)

Comparing against the reference: my **head box is slightly oversized**, the **shin panels slightly too wide**, and the **waist section is simplified** — the reference has a longer articulated chrome column between torso and knees. None of these move a joint centre, so they're safe to fix whenever, which is exactly why the blockout can stay crude.

One honest note: I burned time trying to auto-detect the robot's bounding box for that landmark overlay, and it failed twice (luminance threshold missed the reference's shadowed legs; mine was clipped at the frame edge). Using the *known* extents instead was both simpler and correct. Worth remembering — detection keeps being the wrong tool for things I already know.

## Next

The rig is ready for the step that actually validates it: **match a real reference pose**, not just the neutral stance. The duckwalk at t=75 or the arms-raised frame at t=118 are the candidates — if a static pose matches, the pivots are proven and choreography is safe to key.

For the detection rule:

```md
## Derive, don't detect

**If the answer is derivable from something you already know, derive it. Do not
measure it from pixels.** Detection is for when you genuinely do not know.

Three times on this project, detection was the wrong tool for something already known:

| Wanted | Detected (wrong) | Derived instead |
|---|---|---|
| Our own render's silhouette | thresholded it with computer vision | `scene.overrideMaterial` white-on-black mask pass — exact, free, and it excludes the floor reflection for free too |
| The robot's bounding box, to align a landmark overlay | luminance threshold (missed the shadowed legs) then a background-difference threshold (caught the whole crop, and clipped our own feet) | we know the extents: the reference robot is y 10-690, ours is y 20-700 from the camera maths |
| Which parts are the subject | — | `maskExclude` list on the scene graph |

Detection's real failure mode is not that it is inaccurate. It is that a threshold
always returns *something* plausible-looking, so a wrong answer arrives wearing the
same clothes as a right one. Derivation cannot fail that way: it is either exact or
it throws.

Rule of thumb: reach for detection only when the value is genuinely not representable
in anything you control. Our own render never qualifies — we authored it.
```

```md
## Hierarchies: local offsets are DIFFERENCES

In a joint hierarchy every child offset is `absolute(child) - absolute(parent)`. Using
an absolute height fraction as a parent-relative offset silently places the part far
too high — it put a 2.2m robot's head at 3.15m, and the render just looked "a bit off"
rather than obviously broken. With ~13 joints and more coming, this will recur.

Related quiet footgun: **`Number('')` is `0`, not `NaN`**, so an absent `?bakeTs=`
produced `[0]` and put the page into bake mode instead of playback. When adding a
query parameter, filter empty fields *before* coercing.
```

=== session-format.md (first 60 lines) ===
# Session File Format

Sessions are stored as JSONL (JSON Lines) files. Each line is a JSON object with a `type` field. Session entries form a tree structure via `id`/`parentId` fields, enabling in-place branching without creating new files.

For programmatic creation, persistence, and tree navigation, see the [`SessionManager` API](sdk.md#sessionmanager-api).


## File Location

```
~/.pi/agent/sessions/--<path>--/<timestamp>_<session-id>.jsonl
```

By default, `<session-id>` is a UUID. Callers can supply a custom ID through the SDK or `--session-id`. For `<path>`, Pi removes the leading path separator and replaces `/`, `\\`, and `:` with `-`.

## Deleting Sessions

Sessions can be removed by deleting their `.jsonl` files under `~/.pi/agent/sessions/`.

Pi also supports deleting sessions interactively from `/resume` (select a session and press `Ctrl+D`, then confirm). When available, pi uses the `trash` CLI to avoid permanent deletion.

## Session Version

Sessions have a version field in the header:

- **Version 1**: Linear entry sequence (legacy, auto-migrated on load)
- **Version 2**: Tree structure with `id`/`parentId` linking
- **Version 3**: Renamed `hookMessage` role to `custom` (extensions unification)

Existing sessions are automatically migrated to the current version (v3) when loaded.

## Source Files

Source on GitHub ([pi](https://github.com/earendil-works/pi)):
- [`packages/coding-agent/src/core/session-manager.ts`](https://github.com/earendil-works/pi/blob/main/packages/coding-agent/src/core/session-manager.ts) - Session entry types and SessionManager
- [Message Types](message-types.md) - Shared message and content-block reference
- [`packages/coding-agent/src/core/messages.ts`](https://github.com/earendil-works/pi/blob/main/packages/coding-agent/src/core/messages.ts) - Extended message types
- [`packages/ai/src/types.ts`](https://github.com/earendil-works/pi/blob/main/packages/ai/src/types.ts) - Base message and content-block types
- [`packages/agent/src/types.ts`](https://github.com/earendil-works/pi/blob/main/packages/agent/src/types.ts) - Extensible `AgentMessag
